People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

But when I look at the timestamps and the missing attachments, and how some people won’t answer me straight, it seems obvious to me that someone’s hiding something.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your beliefs about your father's death are possibly mistaken, or do you feel absolutely certain that you are correct?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 34 / computer_systems_analyst / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I know everyone keeps telling me the hospital records and donation emails all line up, but when I compare the timestamps, missing attachments, and the way certain community members avoid my questions, it’s obvious to me that someone is hiding what really happened around my father’s death.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F32.0, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess... I’m here because I can’t let go of some things around my father’s death. People keep saying the hospital records and those donation emails all make sense, but when I look at the timestamps and missing attachments, and how certain people in the community avoid answering me... it doesn’t add up. It feels like someone is hiding what really happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about your father's death and the surrounding circumstances for more than a few hours a day?\n\nPatient: Yeah... probably more than a few hours some days. I try not to, but I keep going back over the records and the emails. The timestamps, the missing attachments, the way some people dodge my questions... it just feels obvious to me that something’s being hidden about what happened to my father.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the hospital or someone in the community is intentionally hiding information about your father's death?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean... I can’t prove every piece yet, but it feels intentional. The records and those donation emails—people say they line up, but the timestamps and missing attachments don’t sit right with me. And when certain people won’t answer simple questions, it’s hard not to think someone’s hiding what really happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think others, besides yourself, also believe that the hospital or community is hiding information about your father's death?\n\nPatient: Not really... I mean, most people tell me it all lines up and I’m reading too much into it. But when I look at the timestamps and the missing attachments, and how some people won’t answer me straight, it seems obvious to me that someone’s hiding something.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about your father's death are possibly mistaken, or do you feel absolutely certain that you are correct?\n\nPatient: I guess people want me to say I could be wrong, but... no, not really. When I compare the timestamps and the missing attachments with how certain people avoid my questions, it seems obvious someone is hiding what really happened.\n\nDoctor: Has your investigation into your father's death and your beliefs about it affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: A little, I guess. My work’s been mostly okay... I still get things done. But with family, some relatives kind of steer away from talking to me now. They know I might bring up the records or what I think was covered up, so they avoid it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts about your father's death are causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t know... it bothers me, obviously. I wouldn’t be looking into it if it didn’t. But I wouldn’t say it’s... ruining me or anything. It’s just there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to continue investigating your father's death, even when you've already looked into it many times?\n\nPatient: Yeah... I mean, I don’t sit there all day with it, but I do keep going back to it.\n\nPeople say the hospital records and the donation emails line up, but when I look at the timestamps and the missing attachments, and then certain people won’t answer me straight... it’s hard to just drop it. It feels like there’s something there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about your father's death would change if you found out that all the missing information you're looking for was actually explained and accounted for by the hospital or the community?\n\nPatient: If everything was really explained, I’d look at it, sure. But... I don’t think that’s what’s happening.\n\nPeople keep saying the hospital records and the donation emails match up, but when I compare the timestamps and see missing attachments, and then certain people in the community avoid answering me directly... it doesn’t feel explained. It feels like someone’s still hiding what actually happened.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about your father's death would be considered strange or unreasonable by most people?\n\nPatient: Most people probably think it’s unreasonable, yeah. But I don’t see it that way.\n\nI know everyone keeps saying the hospital records and the donation emails line up, but when I look at the timestamps, the missing attachments, and how some people avoid my questions... it seems pretty obvious someone’s hiding something about what happened to my father.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your thoughts about your father's death?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say unable to control them. They come up, especially when something reminds me of it, but… I can still do other things. It’s just not something I can completely put away.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your beliefs about your father's death would be considered delusions by a mental health professional?\n\nPatient: They might call it that, I guess. I don’t agree with it.\n\nI know people keep saying the hospital records and the donation emails all match, but when I look at the timestamps, the missing attachments, and how certain people won’t answer me directly... it seems obvious someone’s hiding what really happened with my father.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. They come up, especially when something reminds me of it, but… I can still do other things.
